ChatGPT said:
Executive Summary
SMB restaurants globally operate in a fragmented “public truth layer” where menu, pricing, hours, and availability are inconsistently represented across physical and digital surfaces.
The core issue is not lack of tools, but absence of a canonical source of customer-facing truth.

Most restaurants still manage public information manually across:

Google Business Profile

Delivery platforms

Instagram

PDFs/WhatsApp

Physical menus

QR menus

Websites

This produces daily operational friction, frequent misinformation, and reputational damage.

Key findings:

Menu and pricing inconsistency is the most frequent daily operational problem.

Google/Maps accuracy is the most reputation-sensitive.

Temporary changes (out-of-stock, early closure, festival menus) are the least solved.

Most SMB restaurants still use PDFs, images, or manual messaging as primary truth distribution.

No current tool acts as a reliable, unified public truth controller.

The problem is global and worsening due to platform fragmentation and rising customer expectations for real-time accuracy.

PART 1 — Daily Operational Pain Mapping

1. Menu Version Confusion (No Single Source of Truth)
   Problem
   Restaurants maintain multiple versions of menus (printed, PDF, Google photos, delivery apps, Instagram highlights). These diverge quickly.

Frequency
Daily/weekly.

Current handling

Update printed menu first.

Send new PDF to staff or designer.

Sometimes update delivery apps.

Rarely update Google photos or website consistently.

Why tools fail

POS menus ≠ public menus.

Delivery platforms isolated.

Website builders manual.

Google has no menu version control.

Example
A cafe updates sandwich prices. Printed menu updated same day. Swiggy/Zomato updated next week. Google photos still show old prices for months.

Trend
Growing globally as menus change more frequently due to inflation and supply volatility.

2. Pricing Inconsistency Across Surfaces
   Problem
   Different prices appear across dine-in menu, Google photos, delivery apps, QR menus, and WhatsApp PDFs.

Frequency
Weekly in inflationary markets (India, LATAM). Monthly elsewhere.

Current handling
Manual edits across platforms or ignored.

Why tools fail

No centralized push system.

Delivery platforms often separate pricing.

Google menu images static.

PDF menus widely used but hard to update everywhere.

Impact
Customer disputes at billing counter:

“Google shows ₹220, why are you charging ₹260?”

Trend
Increasing due to rapid ingredient cost changes globally.

3. Item Availability Mismatch
   Problem
   Items shown online but unavailable in-store.

Frequency
Daily.

Current handling

Staff verbally informs customers.

Mark unavailable on delivery apps only.

Ignore Google/website menus.

Why tools fail

No simple real-time public update mechanism.

POS availability changes don’t propagate publicly.

Owners avoid updating multiple platforms.

Example
Breakfast cafe runs out of croissants by 11 AM; still visible on Google/Instagram menu all day.

Trend
Growing as customers rely on Google before visiting.

4. Google/Maps Information Drift
   Problem
   Incorrect hours, phone numbers, menu photos, or closure status on Google.

Frequency
Weekly visibility; monthly corrections.

Current handling

Owners update when complaints appear.

Often delegated to agency.

Sometimes ignored.

Why tools fail

Google edits can be overridden by user suggestions.

Menu photos persist indefinitely.

No system syncing real operational changes.

Impact

Walk-ins arrive during closed hours.

Calls go unanswered if number outdated.

Trend
Growing importance as Google Maps becomes primary discovery surface globally.

5. WhatsApp Menu Sharing Chaos
   Problem
   Customers frequently request menus via WhatsApp.

Frequency
Daily.

Current handling

Send PDF or image manually.

Often outdated file.

Staff sends different versions.

Why tools fail

No live menu link adoption in many SMBs.

PDFs easier culturally.

No controlled distribution source.

Impact
Customer sees outdated price/menu → mismatch on arrival.

Trend
Persistent globally, especially in India, SEA, LATAM.

6. Multi-Language Menu Inconsistency
   Problem
   Tourist-heavy areas maintain English + local language menus that drift apart.

Frequency
Weekly/monthly.

Current handling

Manual translation edits.

One version updated, others not.

Why tools fail

Translation tools not synced to menu changes.

No unified multi-language management.

Example
Paris cafe updates French menu but English version unchanged for months.

Trend
Growing with tourism recovery and global travel.

7. Seasonal/Festival Menu Switching
   Problem
   Temporary menus (Ramadan, Christmas, brunch specials) not reflected everywhere.

Frequency
Monthly/seasonal.

Current handling

Printed inserts.

Instagram posts.

Delivery app update only.

Why tools fail

Switching menus across all surfaces is manual.

Google/website rarely updated for short periods.

Impact
Customers arrive expecting regular menu or special menu incorrectly.

Trend
Increasing as restaurants use limited-time menus more.

8. Temporary Closure & Early Closing
   Problem
   Unexpected closure or early closing not reflected online.

Frequency
Monthly.

Current handling

Instagram story.

Google update (sometimes).

Ignore other platforms.

Why tools fail

No fast universal update mechanism.

Owners don’t update multiple systems for short closures.

Impact

“Google says open but it’s closed.”

Trend
Constant globally.

PART 2 — Multi-Platform Presence Chaos

Conv 2:

You said:
Why Cycles & Loops Are Replacing Funnels in 2026

￼
Justin Brooke
@IMJustinBrooke
·
6h
I built funnels for 20 years.
Hundreds of them.
For myself, for clients, for students at AdSkills. And I'm not going to sit here and tell you they don't work. They do. I've made millions with funnels.
But I've also watched millions leak out the bottom.
That's the thing nobody talks about. You pour people in the top. Some percentage buy. And then what? The rest just... fall out. Gone. You spent real money getting them there. You wrote the ads. You built the landing pages. You set up the email sequences. And for the 95-97% who didn't buy right then? Nothing. You just let them walk away.
I've been guilty of this for years.
We all have.
The Funnel Has a Fatal Flaw
Here's what bugs me. A funnel is a shape that gets smaller. By design. You put a bunch of people in the top and fewer come out the bottom. That's literally what it does. And we named our entire marketing strategy after it.
Think about that for a second.
We built an industry on a model that assumes most people will be lost. We designed for loss. We optimized for loss. We got really, really good at losing people efficiently.
And look, when traffic was cheap and attention was easy to grab, that was fine. You could afford to waste 97% of your leads because the other 3% more than covered the cost. I ran campaigns in the early days where I was buying clicks for pennies. The math worked even with a leaky bucket.
But those days? They're gone. (And they're not coming back.)

￼
Customer acquisition costs have gone through the roof. I've seen niches where a single lead costs $50-150. You can't afford to let those people fall through the cracks anymore. You just can't. The math doesn't work.
Something Better Has Been Brewing
I didn't invent what I'm about to share. I want to be upfront about that. Jim Collins wrote about flywheels in "Good to Great" back in 2001. Jeff Bezos famously sketched Amazon's "virtuous cycle" on a napkin that same year. HubSpot formally killed their own funnel model in 2018 and replaced it with a flywheel.
But here's what I've noticed in my own business and with the students I work with at AdSkills and AgentSkills. The people winning right now aren't the ones with the best funnels. They're the ones who figured out how to turn their marketing into a loop that feeds itself.
And once I started seeing it, I couldn't unsee it.
How a Real Flywheel Works (the Actual Machine)
Stay with me here because this part matters.
A flywheel is a heavy wheel. That's it. A big, heavy disc that spins. They've been around for thousands of years. Potters used them. Steam engines used them. Your car has one sitting between the engine and transmission right now.
Here's what makes them special. When you first start pushing a flywheel, it barely moves. You're shoving with everything you've got and the thing just crawls. It's brutal. You feel like an idiot. You start wondering if it's even worth it.
But you keep pushing.
After a while, it starts to move a little faster. Not much. But you can feel it. Then a little more. And then something shifts. The wheel starts carrying its own momentum. The energy you put in earlier isn't gone. It's stored in the spin. Every push you gave it before is still in there, still working for you. The wheel remembers every effort.
And eventually? The flywheel is screaming. It's moving so fast that your pushes barely add anything compared to the energy already stored in the system. The thing is almost self-sustaining.
That moment when it tips from "pushing a boulder uphill" to "trying to keep up with it rolling downhill." That's the breakthrough. That's what we're after.
And here's the physics that matters for us: if you double the speed of a flywheel, the energy stored doesn't just double. It quadruples. That's not a metaphor. That's actual math. The energy equation is exponential. Small increases in speed create massive increases in stored energy.
Same thing happens in your business when you build a cycle instead of a funnel.
The Marketing Cycle (How It Actually Works)
Forget the funnel shape.
Picture a circle instead. Or better yet, picture that heavy wheel.
Here's the cycle:

￼
Acquire → Educate → Convert → Get Feedback → Optimize → Repeat
And the secret sauce is that last arrow. The one that loops back around.
When you acquire leads, you educate them. Some buy. Great. But the ones who don't buy? They're not lost. They're still in the cycle. They come back around. Maybe they need to see your stuff three more times. Maybe they need to hear a testimonial from someone like them. Maybe they just need six more months to be ready.
The cycle doesn't throw them away. It brings them back.
And the people who DO buy? They're the fuel that makes the next cycle better. You get their testimonials. You get their feedback. You learn what worked and what didn't. You find out what words they use to describe the problem. You discover objections you never thought of.
Then you take all of that and pour it back into the acquire phase.
Better ads. Better content. Better targeting. Because now you're not guessing. You're working with real data from real customers.
Why the First Few Cycles Feel Like Garbage
I'm not going to sugarcoat this. The first two or three cycles of your flywheel are rough. Real rough.
You're pushing that heavy wheel and it's barely moving. You've got no testimonials yet. No customer data to optimize with. No referrals coming in. You're basically still running a funnel at this point, just with a different mindset.
This is where most people quit. (I've seen it a hundred times.)
They try the cycle approach for a month or two. They don't see exponential results yet because the wheel hasn't built momentum. So they bail. They go back to the funnel. They go chase the next shiny traffic source. They hop on the doom loop.

￼
That's what Jim Collins calls it. The doom loop. When you keep switching strategies before any of them have time to build momentum. Push the wheel one direction. Stop. Try another direction. Stop. Try another. You never build any speed because you keep starting over.
I did this myself for longer than I'd like to admit. Jumped from strategy to strategy. Channel to channel. Offer to offer. Every time I switched, I lost all the momentum I'd built.
How to Make the Early Cycles Worth It
OK so here's the part that gets me fired up. Because I think there's a way to make those first few ugly cycles less painful and actually kind of exciting.
Cycle 1: The Learning Cycle
Your first time around, you're not trying to get rich. You're trying to get smart. The whole goal is to push a small group of people through your cycle and learn everything you can from them.
Run a small ad campaign. Get 100-200 leads. Educate them with your best content. Make your offer. See what happens.
Some will buy. Most won't. That's fine. But now you've got gold. You've got data. You've got feedback. Maybe even a testimonial or two. You know which emails got opened. You know which objections came up on sales calls. You know what content they binged.
That's your fuel for Cycle 2.
Cycle 2: The Refinement Cycle
Now you run it again. But this time you've got customer language for your ads. You've got testimonials for your landing pages. You've got data on which content actually moves people. You've tweaked the emails. You've addressed the objections.
Cycle 2 almost always outperforms Cycle 1 by a wide margin. I'm talking 30-50% better conversion rates in my experience. Because you're not guessing anymore. You're iterating.
Cycle 3: The Momentum Cycle
This is where you start to feel it. The wheel is moving. Referrals are trickling in. Your content is getting shared. Your ads are dialed in from two cycles of real data. Your email sequences have been battle-tested.
And here's what nobody tells you. By Cycle 3, your cost per acquisition starts dropping. Because your content is doing work that used to require paid ads. Because your customers are telling their friends. Because your testimonials are doing the selling for you.
The flywheel is starting to carry itself.
Cycle 4 and Beyond: The Compounding Cycles
This is where the magic lives. By now you've got a library of proven content. A bank of testimonials. Referral systems in place. Customer feedback loops that tell you exactly what to build or offer next. You might even have case studies that make your ads write themselves.
Each cycle gets easier. Each cycle converts better. Each cycle costs less to run.
And remember that physics equation from the flywheel? Double the speed, quadruple the energy? That's what's happening in your business now. The growth isn't linear anymore. It's compounding.
Why Funnels Can't Compete With This
Let me paint the picture.
Funnel guy spends $10,000 on ads this month. Gets 1,000 leads. Converts 30 of them. Makes $15,000. Profit: $5,000. Next month? He's gotta spend another $10,000 to do it again. And again. And again. He's on a treadmill.
Cycle guy spends $10,000 on ads the first month too. Gets 1,000 leads. Converts 25 of them. Makes $12,500. Profit: only $2,500. Looks worse, right?
But Cycle guy took those 25 customers and got testimonials, feedback, and referrals. Second month, his ads perform 30% better because they're loaded with social proof and real customer language. His referrals bring in 200 extra leads for free. His email sequences are tighter from what he learned.
Month two: $10,000 in ads gets him 1,300 leads (better ads). Plus 200 referral leads. Converts 60 of them. Makes $30,000.
Month three? Even better. Month six? He's spending the same on ads but getting three times the results because the flywheel is spinning.
Funnel guy is still on the treadmill.
I've lived both of these scenarios. The treadmill one is exhausting. I spent years there. The flywheel one lets you sleep at night.
The 5 Parts of a Cycle That Actually Works
Let me break down what your cycle needs. And I'm keeping this simple on purpose because I've watched too many people overcomplicate this and end up building nothing.

1. Acquire Get people's attention. Ads, content, partnerships, SEO, whatever. But here's the key difference from funnel thinking: you're not just trying to get them to the next step. You're trying to get them into the cycle. Even if they don't buy for six months, they're in the system. They're being nurtured. They're coming back around.
2. Educate Give them real value before you ask for anything. Teach them something. Show them a result. Prove you know what you're talking about. This isn't a tripwire or a free PDF that goes straight to the trash. This is content that actually changes their thinking. Content they'd pay for.
3. Convert Make your offer. Some will buy now. Some will buy later. The point is you're not treating the non-buyers as failures. They're just on a different timeline.
4. Collect Feedback This is where 90% of marketers drop the ball. Talk to your customers. Survey them. Read their emails. Get on calls. Find out what worked, what almost made them leave, what they wish you offered. This is the raw material for everything else.
5. Optimize and Reinvest Take everything you learned and pour it back into Step 1. New testimonials in your ads. Customer language in your copy. Objection-handling in your emails. Case studies in your content. Referral programs for your happy customers.
   Then you run it again. And again. Each time it gets better.

￼
Amazon doesn't run a funnel. They run a cycle. Lower prices attract more customers. More customers attract more sellers. More sellers create more selection. More selection and competition drive prices down even further. And the wheel spins faster.
Bezos literally drew this on a napkin. And built a trillion-dollar company on it.
Dropbox didn't grow through funnels. They built a referral loop. Use the product, share it, your friends sign up, they share it. Their signups jumped 60% from that single loop.
HubSpot killed their own funnel in 2018. Not quietly, either. They wrote a whole blog post about it. Their CEO stood on stage at INBOUND and said the funnel was dead. They replaced it with a flywheel built around customer delight.
These aren't startups experimenting. These are companies worth billions. And they all figured out the same thing: cycles beat funnels.
Your Move
Here's what I want you to do. (And I'm serious about this.)
Look at your current marketing. Find where the cycle breaks. Where are people falling out with no way back in? Where are you ignoring customer feedback? Where are you spending money to acquire leads you already had but lost because you didn't have a loop to bring them back?
That's your starting point.
You don't need to rebuild everything. You need to close the loop.
Start your first cycle. Accept that it'll be ugly. Accept that the first few turns of the flywheel feel like pushing a truck uphill. Accept that Cycle 1 is about learning, not earning.
But keep pushing. Because somewhere around Cycle 3 or 4, you're going to feel it. The momentum. The wheel carrying itself. The growth that used to cost you everything starting to happen on its own.
That's the moment funnel marketers never get to experience. Because they're still on the treadmill, feeding the top, watching the bottom leak.

￼
You won't be.
You'll be spinning.

The “cycles vs funnels” framing is directionally correct — but incomplete.

Funnels optimize conversion efficiency per cohort.

Below is the structural breakdown — not the marketing version.

1. Funnels Optimize Extraction. Cycles Optimize Retention of Energy.
   A funnel is a one-pass system:

Traffic → Lead → Offer → Purchase → End

Its core assumption:
You can always buy more attention.

That assumption breaks when:

CAC rises faster than LTV

Platform arbitrage disappears

Audiences fragment

Ad fatigue accelerates

Funnels leak by design. They are mathematically dependent on fresh input.

A cycle (flywheel) is a closed energy system:

Acquire → Deliver Value → Capture Signal → Reinforce → Re-acquire

The difference is structural:

Funnels convert people.

Cycles convert data, proof, and momentum.

That is why cycles compound.

2. The Real Economic Shift: CAC Inflation + Attention Scarcity
   When:

A lead costs $50–150

CPMs rise

Organic reach decays

Trust declines

You cannot afford linear economics.

If you acquire someone and fail to:

Capture language

Capture objections

Capture testimonials

Capture behavioral signals

Capture referrals

You destroyed asset value.

A funnel treats non-buyers as waste.

A cycle treats every interaction as retained signal capital.

That’s the difference.

3. The Physics Metaphor Is Correct — But Here’s the Real Mechanism
   The “flywheel stores energy” analogy is useful, but what’s actually compounding?

Three assets:

1. Trust Density
   Testimonials

Case studies

Public proof

Social validation

Trust reduces friction in future acquisition.

2. Message Accuracy
   Real customer language

Objection clarity

Behavioral insight

Channel optimization

Each loop improves targeting precision.

3. Organic Inflow
   Referrals

Word-of-mouth

User-generated content

Repeat buyers

Paid acquisition dependency declines over time.

When these three reinforce each other, CAC drops while CVR rises.

That’s the flywheel.

4. Why Most “Cycle” Attempts Fail
   Because people misdiagnose what a loop actually is.

Common failure modes:

They bolt email retargeting onto a funnel and call it a flywheel.

They don’t structurally capture feedback.

They never operationalize testimonials.

They don’t feed learnings back into acquisition.

They switch strategy before momentum forms (doom loop).

A true cycle requires:

Signal Capture Infrastructure

Proof Amplification Infrastructure

Iteration Discipline

Time

Without those, it’s just a prettier funnel diagram.

5. Examples Often Cited (And What They Actually Did)
   Jim Collins
   Described momentum systems in Good to Great — not marketing funnels, but organizational compounding.

Jeff Bezos + Amazon
Their loop:
Lower prices → More customers → More sellers → Better selection → Lower prices.

That is structural economics, not marketing tactics.

HubSpot
Shifted from funnel to flywheel centered on customer delight because SaaS retention economics dominate one-time acquisition.

Dropbox
Referral loop embedded into product architecture. The product itself generated acquisition.

Notice the pattern:
The loop is embedded into the system, not layered on top.

6. Funnels Still Work — But Only in Specific Contexts
   Funnels remain effective when:

Product is low-ticket and impulsive

Traffic is cheap

Purchase cycle is short

Brand trust isn’t required

LTV is front-loaded

They fail when:

Sales cycles are long

Trust is required

Market is saturated

CAC is high

Retention drives profitability

In 2026, most serious businesses fall into the second category.

7. The Strategic Difference
   Funnel mindset

How do I convert this click?

How do I increase this page’s CVR?

How do I scale spend?

Cycle mindset

How do I reduce CAC over 12 months?

How do I turn buyers into acquisition assets?

How do I reduce paid dependency?

How do I turn feedback into conversion leverage?

Funnels optimize revenue.
Cycles optimize enterprise value.

That’s why cycles are replacing funnels.

8. The Hard Truth
   Cycles are slower at the beginning.
   They look worse in month one.
   They require patience.
   They demand system thinking.
   They punish strategy switching.

Most marketers don’t lack tactics.
They lack endurance.

That’s why funnels dominated for 20 years — they give immediate feedback.

But compounding businesses are not built on immediate feedback.
They’re built on retained energy.

Design a structural flywheel from first principles

Or pressure-test whether cycles even apply to your business model

Different models require different physics.

_Deep Research Report: Real Daily Operational & Public-Truth Problems Faced by SMB Restaurants_

_Executive Summary_

SMB restaurants face chronic, structural problems with maintaining accurate public business information across multiple platforms. These issues create daily operational friction, damage customer trust, and waste significant owner time. Current solutions fail because they treat information as static data rather than dynamic operational reality. The problems are universal across markets but manifest differently based on platform ecosystems and regulatory environments.

───────────
_PART 1 — DAILY OPERATIONAL PAIN MAPPING_

_1. Menu Management Chaos (Daily/Weekly)_
_Problem_: Owners manually update menus across 5-10 platforms whenever items change, prices adjust, or seasonal items are added/removed. Updates happen multiple times daily during peak seasons.

_Current Handling_: Manual updates via spreadsheets, WhatsApp messages to staff, phone calls to delivery partners, and individual platform logins. Average update time: 2-3 hours per change.

_Tool Failure_: POS systems update inventory but don't sync to external platforms. Menu management tools require manual content uploads. Delivery platforms have separate interfaces with different update cycles.

_Example_: A Bangalore café discovered their "cold coffee" was priced at ₹250 on Zomato but ₹280 on Swiggy after a price increase, leading to customer complaints and negative reviews.

_Trend_: _Growing_ - Platform fragmentation increases daily as new delivery services and social commerce emerge.

_2. Google Business Profile Inconsistencies (Weekly)_
_Problem_: Hours, menus, and contact information become outdated within days of physical changes, creating customer confusion.

_Current Handling_: Owners log into Google Business Profile quarterly, cross-reference with 3-4 other platforms, and make incremental updates.

_Tool Failure_: Google's update propagation takes 24-72 hours across all services. No real-time sync with POS or website changes. Manual verification still required.

_Example_: A Delhi restaurant closed for Diwali but Google still showed them as open, resulting in 50+ customer complaints and angry calls.

_Trend_: _Stable_ - Platform-specific issues persist despite Google's improvements.

_3. Multi-Platform Pricing Drift (Daily/Weekly)_
_Problem_: Same item costs ₹20-50 more on delivery apps vs. physical menu, creating perceived price gouging.

_Current Handling_: Manual price reconciliation twice daily, often with different staff members handling different platforms, leading to human error.

_Tool Failure_: Each delivery platform's pricing system is isolated. Automatic syncing exists only between POS and website, not delivery platforms. Commission-based pricing models create structural misalignment.

_Example_: Mumbai restaurant chain discovered Zomato charging 15% more than Swiggy for the same thali, confusing regular customers who use both platforms.

_Trend_: _Growing_ - As platforms add dynamic pricing and promotional overlays.

_4. WhatsApp Menu Management (Weekly/Monthly)_
_Problem_: Menus shared via WhatsApp become outdated within days but customers continue using them for ordering.

_Current Handling_: Managers manually track which customers have old menus, send corrections via broadcast lists, and maintain separate current/previous menu versions.

_Tool Failure_: No automated version control or distribution system. WhatsApp's broadcast limits and message deletion policies create additional friction.

_Example_: Chennai restaurant's lunch special menu was shared on WhatsApp on Monday, but price changed on Wednesday. 30% of orders on Friday were still based on old prices.

_Trend_: _Growing_ - WhatsApp business adoption increasing, but management tools lagging.

_5. Seasonal Menu Switching (Monthly)_
_Problem_: Seasonal items require complete menu restructuring across all platforms, often during peak business periods.

_Current Handling_: Create new menu PDFs, manually update each platform, and hope customers don't notice old menus still indexed.

_Tool Failure_: No dynamic seasonal menu system. Platforms don't support "seasonal override" functionality that temporarily modifies base menu.

_Example_: Lucknow restaurant's Ramzan special menu took 3 days to propagate across platforms, missing the first week of Iftar rush.

_Trend_: _Stable_ - Seasonal business cycles consistent, tools not improving.

10. _Payment Method Updates_ - Monthly, temporary confusion, low impact

_Current Workaround Behaviors_

• _Spreadsheet Hell_: Maintaining separate spreadsheets for each platform's data
• _WhatsApp Broadcast Chains_: Manually distributing updated menus via messaging
• _Phone Tag_: Calling delivery partners multiple times daily
• _Email Chains_: Coordinating changes across teams through email
• _Manual Screenshots_: Taking menu screenshots and uploading to all platforms
• _Customer Education_: Training staff to explain information discrepancies

_Why Existing Tools Fail_

• _Siloed Architecture_: Each platform operates independently with no communication
• _Manual Integration Requirements_: No automated cross-platform synchronization
• _Static Data Approach_: Tools treat menus as documents, not dynamic operational data
• _Limited API Access_: Platforms restrict real-time data access
• _No Version Control_: No rollback or change tracking capabilities
• _Poor Mobile Experience_: Desktop-focused interfaces for on-the-go restaurant managers

_Structural Gaps in Industry_

• _No Central Truth System_: No single source of truth that propagates automatically
• _No Real-Time Communication_: Platforms don't communicate with each other
• _No Dynamic Menu Architecture_: Menus treated as static files rather than dynamic systems
• _No Automated Change Management_: No system for handling temporary or seasonal changes
• _No Customer Communication Integration_: No automated notification system for information changes

_Most Critical Unsolved Problems_

1. _Real-Time Cross-Platform Synchronization_ - The fundamental problem requiring architectural solution
2. _Dynamic Menu Architecture_ - Need for menu system that can handle multiple versions and contexts
3. _Automated Change Management_ - System for handling temporary changes and closures
4. _Customer Communication Integration_ - Automated notification system for information changes
5. _Price Rule Management_ - Centralized system for managing complex pricing across platforms

_Future Trend Prediction (Next 5 Years)_

_Year 1-2_: Increased API standardization but still fragmented implementations
_Year 2-3_: Emergence of middleware solutions attempting to solve synchronization
_Year 3-4_: Platform consolidation reduces complexity but creates new integration challenges
_Year 4-5_: AI-powered dynamic menu systems that automatically adapt to real-time conditions
_Year 5+_: Full ecosystem integration with predictive capabilities and automated change management

The industry will eventually require fundamental architectural changes rather than better point solutions. The current fragmented approach is unsustainable as platform complexity and customer expectations continue to grow.

Every SaaS Is Now an API. Whether They Like It or Not.

￼

·
Feb 23
I don't log into any of my SaaS anymore. My agent does.
Not Brex. Not QuickBooks. Not HubSpot. Not Mixpanel. Not Datadog. Not Gmail. Not Stripe. My agent navigates all of them for me.
Every piece of software my company uses, I access through a single AI agent connected to their APIs. Banking, accounting, CRM, product analytics, infrastructure monitoring, meeting notes, LLM evals. All headless. All through natural language. The agent fetches the data, merges context across systems, creates Excel and PowerPoint artifacts, asks me for decisions when it needs them, and builds its own system of record: decision logs, daily changelogs, structured memory files that capture how I work and why I made the calls I made.
It gives me superpowers. Before Fintool, I had a company with 100+ people. I had a VP of Legal, a VP of HR, a VP of Finance. Today I run Fintool with a team of six and I handle more than I ever did with a hundred people and a full leadership team. Not because I work harder. Because the agent absorbs the operational load that used to require an entire back office.
When I published my piece on vertical SaaS and the collapsing interface moat, the most common pushback was: "I prefer UI. I don't like chat." Fair enough. But they're picturing ChatGPT. ChatGPT is not an agentic product. The only people who have felt the real agentic future are heavy users of Claude Code, Claude Cowork, or OpenClaw. Everyone else is operating with a 2024 mental model.
This post is a walkthrough of what that future actually looks like and it's really fun!
In this article:
• Why people judge AI by ChatGPT and why that's like judging the internet by AOL
• My actual daily workflow: every SaaS product, accessed through an agent, never through their interface
• Why headless is better: the power of merging context across sources
• How my agent builds memory files that make it smarter every day (system of record)
• A real story from my fundraise that shows what this looks like in practice
• Why every SaaS is now an API, and what happens to those that don't have one (WebMCP, browser agents, and the end of the UI moat)
• Business to Agent (B2A): the most underrated shift in enterprise software
• Why some SaaS are heading toward perfect competition
My Setup: Every SaaS Product, Zero Dashboards
Let me walk you through what my actual day looks like.
I use Claude Code as a general purpose agent harness. Not as a chatbot. As an operating system for running my company. It's connected via API to every tool I use:

￼
Brex for banking. QuickBooks for accounting. HubSpot for CRM. Gmail for email. Stripe for invoicing and payments. Mixpanel for product analytics. Datadog for infrastructure monitoring. Braintrust for LLM evals. Granola for meeting notes etc etc.
I do not log into any of these products. I talk to my agent and it talks to them.
When I want to know how a customer is doing, I don't open HubSpot, then open Mixpanel, then open QuickBooks in three different browser tabs and try to piece together the story. I say: "Give me a full picture of Kennedy Capital. Pull their deal history from HubSpot, their product usage from Mixpanel, their invoicing and payment status from Stripe, and any recent support threads from Gmail." The agent goes to four different APIs, fetches everything, merges the context, and gives me one coherent answer.
The power of headless isn't avoiding dashboards. It's merging context from sources that were never designed to talk to each other.
No human can hold the full context of CRM, analytics, payments, and email in working memory at the same time. The agent can. And when it has all that context simultaneously, the quality of its analysis is fundamentally different from what you get by looking at each system individually.
The interaction model is not just text in, text out. When the agent hits a decision point, it surfaces a structured question with options for me to choose from:

￼
This is UI. It's just UI that the agent assembled dynamically from four data sources in ten seconds. No SaaS company on earth builds a dashboard that shows you HubSpot deal status + Mixpanel usage trends + Stripe invoicing + Gmail threads in one view. But the agent does. Because it has access to all of them through APIs.
People who say "I prefer a UI to text" haven't seen this. They're imagining typing into ChatGPT and getting a wall of text back. That's not what this is. This is a dynamic interface that's better than any dashboard because it's not limited to one vendor's data.
Why Headless Is Better: Context Merging and Memory
The single biggest advantage of working through an agent is something no SaaS dashboard will ever give you: merged context across every system your business runs on.
When I ask my agent a question, it doesn't just query one system. It queries all of them and reasons over the combined data. This is categorically different from what any individual SaaS product can offer. Mixpanel can show me product analytics. But it can't tell me which of my dropping-usage accounts also has an overdue invoice in Stripe and a stalled deal in HubSpot. The agent can, because it sees everything.
But context merging is just the beginning. The second superpower is memory.
I've instructed my agent to maintain detailed memory files about how I work. It writes a daily changelog of every action it takes: what it queried, what decisions I made, what the outcomes were. It maintains a folder of key decisions and the reasoning behind them. It tracks my work preferences: how I like reports formatted, which metrics I care about, how I evaluate new tools, my communication style.

￼
Over time, this makes the agent dramatically better. It doesn't just know what I asked today. It knows what I decided last month, why I decided it, and how I like to approach similar problems. It's building institutional knowledge that normally lives in the heads of a lot of different people. Except it's in files, it's searchable, and it never forgets.
At Doctrine, institutional knowledge lived in people. When my VP of Finance left, his understanding of our billing quirks, our investor reporting preferences, our accounting edge cases, all of that walked out the door with him. With an agent that maintains structured memory files, that knowledge persists. It compounds.
This is why I work headless. Not because I hate UIs. Because the combination of cross-system context merging and persistent memory is so much more powerful than any single SaaS dashboard that going back feels like using a calculator after you've had a spreadsheet.
A Real Example: The Fundraising Due Diligence Problem
Let me make this concrete.
When I was raising money for Fintool, our investors flagged an issue during due diligence. We had non-voided invoices in Stripe that were being counted as bad debt in the books. It looked like we had receivables we couldn't collect. In reality, these were invoices that should have been voided but weren't. A bookkeeping cleanup issue, not a business problem. But to prove that to investors, I needed to pull the full picture across multiple systems.
Before I had my agent set up, here's what that looked like: log into Stripe, pull all outstanding invoices, export to Excel. Log into QuickBooks, pull the accounting records for the same period, export to Excel. Log into Brex, pull the bank transactions to confirm which payments actually settled, export to Excel. Log into HubSpot, pull the deal records for full context on each customer. Open Excel. Manually cross-reference four spreadsheets. Build a reconciliation table. Format it for investors.
This took me the better part of a day. And the output was mediocre because I was tired and error prone by hour four.

￼
After I connected my agent to all four APIs, the same task took five minutes. One sentence. The agent pulled data from all four systems, cross-referenced everything, asked me one clarifying question ("This customer has two accounts in HubSpot, which one is the active account?"), and produced a clean Excel file I could send directly to investors.
The output was better than what I would have done manually. Because the agent had perfect context across all four systems simultaneously. Something I can't hold in working memory when I'm flipping between browser tabs and spreadsheets at midnight before a board meeting.
The magic isn't the chat. It's the context across systems.
Every SaaS Is Now an API: The Strategic Shift
So here's the question every SaaS founder should be asking: if the future is agents talking to software through APIs, what happens to the software that doesn't have a good API? Or worse, doesn't have one at all?
Three things are happening simultaneously. And together, they mean that every piece of SaaS is becoming an API endpoint, voluntarily or not.

1. If you have a good API, agents will use it
   This is the best case. The agent calls your API, gets clean structured data back, and your product becomes a first-class participant in agentic workflows. Your customers love you because their agent works seamlessly with your product. This is Brex. This is Stripe. This is QuickBooks. Their APIs are clean, fast, well documented. My agent works beautifully with them.
2. If you don't have an API, WebMCP is coming for you
   Google Chrome just shipped WebMCP (Web Model Context Protocol) in early preview with Chrome 146 Canary. This is a proposed web standard, developed jointly by Google and Microsoft through the W3C, that lets any website expose structured, callable tools directly to AI agents through the browser.
   The implication is massive. Instead of scraping your website or taking screenshots, an agent can call structured functions that your website exposes. A single WebMCP tool call replaces what used to be dozens of browser interactions: clicking filters, scrolling pages, parsing results.
   This means that even if you deliberately choose not to build an API, the browser itself will turn your website into one. WebMCP is designed to become the USB-C of agent interactions with the web. One standardized interface that any agent can plug into. If this becomes a web standard (and with Google and Microsoft co-authoring it, it probably will), every website becomes an API whether the company intended it or not.
3. Worst case: agents just browse your site anyway
   Even without an API and without WebMCP, agents can already use browser automation to navigate your product. They take screenshots, identify buttons, click through forms, and extract data. It's slow, expensive, and fragile. But it works. And it's getting better every month.
   So the gradient looks like this:│

￼
The UI moat is collapsing no matter what you do. The only question is whether you participate in that collapse on your terms (good API) or on someone else's (WebMCP, scraping).
Your interface is no longer your product. Your data model and your API are.
Let me make this concrete. I use Rippling for HR and payroll. The UI is painful. My team hates using it. But that's almost beside the point now. The real problem is that Rippling doesn't open their API easily. My agent connects to Brex and QuickBooks seamlessly. It can pull financial data, reconcile invoices, generate reports. But the moment a workflow touches payroll data, everything breaks. The agent can't get clean data out of Rippling. So every workflow that needs HR information, headcount costs for budgeting, payroll data for investor reporting, employee information for compliance, I have to do it manually. Log into Rippling. Navigate the terrible UI. Export a CSV. Paste it into the workflow.
The whole flow breaks because one vendor doesn't have a usable API. And it's not just annoying. It's a purchasing decision. I am actively looking to move to a payroll provider that's API first. Not because Rippling's features are bad. Not because a competitor gave a better demo. Because my agent can't work with Rippling, and that makes the product useless in the way I actually use software now.
API quality is now a primary purchasing criterion. If your API fails the agent, you lose the customer.
Business to Agent (B2A): The New Growth Motion
This brings us to what I think is the most underrated shift in enterprise software: the move from B2B to B2A. Business to Agent.
In the B2B world, software is sold to humans. A VP of Finance sits in a demo, clicks around a dashboard, evaluates the UX, compares it to competitors, runs a procurement process, and signs a contract.
In the B2A world, software is consumed by agents. The agent evaluates the API, not the UI. The agent cares about latency, reliability, data richness, and documentation. It doesn't need onboarding. It doesn't care about your NPS.

￼
This is already how I evaluate tools at Fintool. When we were looking at new software, I didn't sit through demos. I connected Claude Code to each product's API and ran real workflows. The software that produced clean, fast, structured results won. The one where the agent couldn't get data out (Rippling) is getting replaced.
No API means you're done. Not eventually. Now. Having an API is just table stakes. The real competition is API quality: speed, reliability, data richness, documentation, error handling. Because agents develop preferences. Or more precisely, the developers building agent workflows develop preferences, and those preferences calcify into defaults. If your API is slow or badly structured, the agent picks a competitor. Silently. Every time.
The agent becomes the buyer. The agent recommends. The human approves.
This inverts the entire enterprise sales motion. You're no longer selling to humans through beautiful UI. You're selling to agents through excellent APIs.
The Commoditization Risk: SaaS Meets Perfect Competition?
Here's where it gets brutal for software companies.
When the UI moat collapses and everything becomes an API, SaaS starts to look like a commodity market.
Think about what historically justified premium SaaS pricing. A beautiful interface that users spent years learning. Workflow automations that encoded how an entire organization operates. Integrations that were painful to rebuild. Training costs. Migration costs. All of these created switching costs. And switching costs are what allow you to charge a lot per year.
When agents mediate the software experience, every single one of those switching costs collapses.
The agent doesn't have years of muscle memory on your interface. It doesn't care about your workflow automations because it builds its own. It doesn't need training or migration support. From the agent's perspective, switching from HubSpot to Attio is pointing to a different API endpoint. It takes seconds.

￼
When switching costs collapse, pricing power follows.
This is closer to the economics of a utility than a traditional SaaS business. Think Twilio, not Salesforce. Twilio's messaging API sells at thiner margins than regular software. Salesforce sells a UI, a workflow, a platform, at premium prices with 75%+ gross margins.
When agents reduce every SaaS product to its API, you're potentially moving from Salesforce economics to Twilio economics. Not every SaaS will get there. Companies with genuinely proprietary data, regulatory lock-in, or transaction embedding will hold pricing power. But for the many SaaS products whose moat was primarily UI and workflow? The compression is real.
SaaS pricing compression is coming. Driven not by competing sales teams, but by agents making frictionless comparisons and switches on behalf of users. Great for buyers. Brutal for incumbents who built their moat in UI rather than data and API quality.
What To Do If You're Building SaaS Today
Audit your API surface. Not the one in your docs. The real one. Is it fast enough for an agent to call in a multi-step workflow? Is the data model clean enough for an LLM to reason over? Are error messages clear enough for an agent to self-correct?
Think about auth and permissioning for agents, not just humans. Today, API keys are designed for developers building integrations. Tomorrow, every user will have an agent that needs scoped access to their account. You need machine-to-machine auth patterns and audit trails that make sense for agent actions.
Make API quality a first-class product metric. Latency, reliability, uptime, data freshness. These matter more than your NPS score now. An agent that gets a 500 error doesn't leave a bad review on G2. It switches to a competitor and never comes back.
Prepare for WebMCP. If you have a web application, start thinking about what tools you'd expose through the WebMCP standard. Which functions would an agent want to call? What data would it need? Get ahead of this before the browser turns your site into an API you didn't design.
Watch for agent-native entrants in your market. Someone is building the API-first version of your product right now. They're small. They won't be for long.
Don't wait for the shift to be obvious. By then you're already behind.
The Agentic Era Is Already Here
At my previous company, I had 100+ people. A VP of Legal, a VP of HR, a VP of Finance. Every one of them spent their days operating specialized software through specialized interfaces. Logging into dashboards. Running reports. Exporting spreadsheets. Stitching data together manually.
Today at Fintool, I have more agents than people. The agent pulls my Stripe invoices, reconciles them against QuickBooks accounting records and Brex bank transactions, checks Mixpanel for product usage trends, monitors Datadog for infrastructure health, and maintains memory files that capture every key decision and why we made it. It does in minutes what used to take a leadership team days.
I'm replacing payroll with tokens.
The people who push back and say "I prefer UI" are right to want a good interface. But the best interface is no longer a static dashboard designed by one SaaS vendor. It's an agent that dynamically assembles the perfect view from every system in your business, asks you for the decisions that matter, and handles everything else.
The question is no longer whether agents will run business workflows. They already do. The future is here; it's just unevenly distributed>
The question is whether your SaaS will be a first-class participant in that world, with a clean, fast, well-documented API that agents love to work with, or whether it will be the Rippling in someone's stack: the weak link that gets scraped, bypassed, and eventually replaced by a competitor that understood what "every SaaS is now an API" actually means.

Claude and Manus both released connectors for Meta Ads. Claude still can't make changes in ad accounts — it can only do analysis, and it has no access to Google Ads. But give it a few months, and it will. So building here feels pointless.
Like, every marketer will have Claude Cowork, ask "launch me a million-dollar ad campaign. make no mistakes" and go go go.
But what's next?
We need to think about the overall future of the GTM stack.
Here are the big GTM categories today:
1 Own data — CRMs will be fine. Claude can't store your customer data.
2 Lead databases — Clay, Apollo, RB2B will be fine. Claude can't collect 1B phone numbers and emails.
3 Outreach automation — dead soon. You'll be able to tell Claude "Spam all YC founders offering my SaaS" and it'll just do it.
4 Outreach infra — also dead. Agents will soon buy domains and set up outreach automation on their own.
5 Niche tools — server-side tracking and similar will survive.
6 Creatives — mostly dead. In a few months Meta and Google will generate them inside ad accounts. There will be some players who do top-tier large volume creatives for huge advertisers, but for small businesses it'll be inside Google/Meta.
7 Enterprise complex workflows — like CDPs, customer engagement for huge brands. They are safe and will be doing exceedingly well. And waiting for more cool players in the space.
What's coming for marketing and distribution
AI made building free and distribution harder than ever. Everyone ships now. Good luck getting anyone to notice. Current GTM tech is pre-agentic era — it's not up to speed with where things are going. It can't sell to agents. But I'm a huge believer in the future of this space.
When building is free, distribution becomes everything — and that means more money, attention and energy will flow here than ever.
A few predictions:
• MCP is the new App Store. Claude picks a tool via MCP. No marketplace, no comparison — the AI picks one tool and the user never sees alternatives. This is the iPhone App Store in July 2008.
• Ads inside LLMs are the next trillion-dollar channel. Someone will build the ad network that connects thousands of chatbots the way Google connected websites. That company will be generational.
• Agentic commerce is coming. AI agents don't pay extra for brand. They compare specs, read reviews, pick the best value. Every category races toward commodity pricing.
• AI-to-AI sales will break enterprise playbooks. AI agents will read docs, compare specs, analyze pricing. They don't watch demos or get charmed.
• Social: completely botted and hijacked. The best GTM people know how to work the algorithms and get billions of views for free. It's a volume game once you figure out how to create decent, engaging content. Very soon — if not already — 98% of TikTok and Instagram will be fake AI content boosted by bots to go viral.
• Brand is more important than ever. It's almost impossible to replicate with AI. Last moat for selling to humans: story, trust, taste.
• The gap between good and bad marketers will explode. Anyone can generate AI slop for ~$0. It doesn't work. Taste, viral instincts, and storytelling are your alpha.
• Marketing is still a volume game. Try every channel, scale what works, do 10,000x of it. The tools die, but the hustle doesn't. We need human hustlers who can test everything and scale it.
What we're going to do next as a team
Our current business will be fine anyway. We knew what was coming and started actively pivoting a few weeks ago. Now we also build complex workflows for huge ad agencies managing 100s of accounts — like some of our clients manage 600 accounts with 6 people. Pretty insane.
We also sell AI-native ad agency services to SMBs because we know how to sell to small gas stations in Texas type of businesses, and they love how our AI and humans genuinely care about them.
But I definitely want to think more about the future of distribution in the post-Claude era and what cool and great things we can build here. Stay tuned.

Conv 6:

You said:
The traditional SaaS is over → I think this is what comes next

￼
John Rush
@johnrushx
·
1h
Over the past 30 days, 99% of my habits have changed.
Instead of logging in to Stripe, my bank, Gmail, and 30 other SaaS tools, I simply got everything I needed through an AI agent connected to all of them via MCPs.
I wake up, open one chat, and say, "Show me yesterday's revenue, any failed payments, and all questions and bug reports from my customers." Done. No 14 tabs. No context switching. No remembering which dashboard has which chart.
The key reason I chose the products I did was their UX. On the database level, all products are pretty much the same, or even if different, they cover the same use cases.
The Traditional UI was basically a layer between me and the database, and now I can use AI Chat to do reads/writes/updates/deletes.
In cases where I wanna see a chart or a table or anything advanced, I ask AI for it, and it either generates it on the fly or I ask it to generate a little tool for this use case. So I ended up vibe coding a super dashboard that works literally with all the tools I use, all in one, only the things I need.
Here's the thing most people don't realize: I only use 5% of any given tool. The rest was just noise to me. So I just picked the 5% from each and put it all into one dashboard.
Think about that. 95% of every SaaS product you're paying for is features you'll never click on. And now you don't have to.
The UX Moat Is Dead
All this made me think: if UX isn't a MOAT anymore, then I don't really care who is the provider of the API...
Whoever gives me a better price for any of the tools, I'll take it.
I'd even want my agent to take care of this: every day it'd check if there are cheaper alternatives to the tools I use, and if there are, it'd cancel the existing tool, download all my data, and transfer it to the new tool.
No migration headaches.
This obviously means that all such tools gonna have their race to the bottom and reach price zero. And I wouldn't even know what tools I'm using. Their brands wouldn't stick in my mind, because I don't care...
I don't care if my email goes through Resend or Postmark or SES. I don't care if my database is Supabase or PlanetScale or Neon. I just care that the thing works and costs the least.
Brand loyalty is gone. Switching costs are gone.
What's The MOAT Then?
Perhaps the LLM models. Opus 4.6 beats everything else right now, so I'd not switch and I'm willing to pay whatever they ask.
But that game is for the big guys, so it's out of my and your mind as founders.
What's left then?
At first glance... nothing, actually. Tech has no moat. Tech has no defensibility anymore.
Tech business is basically going from 99% profit margins to 1%.
Every SaaS tool is becoming a commodity. Every API is interchangeable. Every database does the same thing. And the AI agent sitting in front of all of them doesn't care about your landing page, your onboarding flow, your clever feature naming, or your freemium funnel.
If you're building a CRUD app with a pretty UI and calling it a SaaS business... yeah, you should be worried. That era is ending.

WHAT COMES NEXT!

The Agent Is The Product
Think about what I did. I collapsed 30 tools into one chat interface. I built my own command center. I only see the 5% that matters to me from each tool.
Cool. But I'm a technical founder who's been shipping products for years. What about everyone else?
Someone needs to do this for them. The agent layer. The orchestration layer.
I basically built this for myself already. The question is: can you productize it?
I think you can. And I think that's where the next wave of billion-dollar companies comes from.
Full repos with one-time payment
For most of my vibe coding projects, I'd happily pay someone $199 to purchase the code that I'll then throw into Claude Code and ask to customize it for my needs, and basically, instead of starting from zero, I start with a good default.
The market for such code repositories will be pretty big. The recurring payment will be gone, but selling 10k copies for $199 each every month will equal $2M/mo, and it's pretty achievable (since most users gonna stop paying monthly for their saas, paying once for such repos gonna be an easy decision)
Big Data (this buzzword finally means smth)
Here's something interesting I noticed. My agent can switch me from Resend to SES in 5 minutes. It can move my database from Supabase to Neon overnight. It can cancel my Stripe account and set me up on a competitor.
But it can't download the collective intelligence of millions of users and bring it with me.
Like... Stripe's fraud detection. It's trained on billions of transactions across millions of businesses. That data IS the product. My agent can't just replicate that by switching to a cheaper payment processor.
If your product genuinely gets better because more people use it... that's the one thing agents can't commoditize. Everything else has no MOAT.
Boring Regulated Stuff
My agent auto-switches my email provider without me knowing. Would I let it auto-switch my bank? My healthcare provider? My insurance underwriter? NO!
The barrier to entry isn't code, it's licenses, compliance, and trust.
Sell The Result, Not The Tool
I don't wanna pay $49/month for a social media management tool anymore. I used maybe 3 features out of 200. And now my agent does those 3 things anyway.
But what I WOULD pay for: "we'll get you 50 qualified leads this month from LinkedIn." That's not a tool, that's a result. And I don't care how you do it. I don't care what APIs you use behind the scenes. I just care that it works.
The pricing model flips from "pay for access" to "pay for outcomes."
You can actually charge MORE than SaaS. Because you're not competing on features or price. You're competing on "did it work or not."
My agent will optimize my tool spending to zero. But it'll happily pay for results all day long, because results have a clear ROI.
The Punchline
Yeah, tech margins are going to zero. For most SaaS, the game is over.
But every time I've seen something die in tech, the thing that replaces it is 10x bigger. Websites killed newspapers, but created Google. Mobile killed websites, but created the app economy. AI agents are killing SaaS, but they're creating... something we don't even have a name for yet.
So stop building tools. Start building what comes after.

2. High-level architecture
   You need three layers:

Layer A — Current state (fast read layer)

stores

menus

categories

items

storeSettings

fast reads

rendering

editing

Never overwrite
Never delete

3. Event-first architecture (critical shift)
   Stop thinking:

save final state

Start thinking:

record event → then update state

Every mutation must generate event.

Example flow:

User edits item price
→ create event log entry
→ update item document
Event log is primary truth history.

4. Core event types to implement
   Keep tight and structured.

A. Item-level events (most important)
item_created
Fields:

storeId

itemId

categoryId

initial price

timestamp

source (manual/import/AI)

item_updated
Trigger whenever:

name change

price change

description change

image change

availability change

changedFields (array)

oldValues

newValues

timestamp

item_deleted
Track:

timestamp

last known price

lifetime duration

B. Price history events (separate explicit log)
Even if inside item_updated, create explicit price event.

item_price_changed
Fields:

storeId

itemId

oldPrice

newPrice

currency

timestamp

Why separate?

C. Availability events
item_availability_changed
Track:

available → unavailable

unavailable → available

temporary disable

timestamp

real-world availability behavior dataset.

D. Category/menu structure events
category_created
category_deleted
category_renamed
item_moved_category
menu_reordered
section_restructured
Store:

before

after

timestamp

global menu structure dataset.

menu_published
Fields:

storeId

menuVersion

itemsChangedCount

categoriesChangedCount

timestamp

publishSource (manual/auto)

publish_correction
If publish followed by quick fix:

timestamp

correction type

operational discipline + reliability.

hours_changed
phone_changed
location_changed
store_status_changed
Track all.

business reliability graph.

5. Firestore schema design (practical)
   Use flat event collections for scalability.

Option A — single global event stream (preferred)
menuEvents (collection)

docId: auto
{
tId
sId
entityType: "item" | "category" | "store" | "menu"
entityId
eventType: "item_price_changed"
oldValue: {}
newValue: {}
changedFields: []
timestamp
actor: "owner" | "ai" | "system"
source: "editor" | "import" | "api"
}
Benefits:

simple

scalable

queryable globally

future ML ready

Option B — per-store subcollection (acceptable)
stores/{storeId}/events/{eventId}
Better for isolation.
Harder for global analysis later.

Recommendation:
Use global event collection + storeId index.

   Every publish should create:

menu_snapshot
menuSnapshots
{
storeId
version
fullMenuJson (compressed)
itemCount
timestamp
}

historical accuracy

evolution patterns

John Rush

1h
Over the past 30 days, 99% of my habits have changed.
Instead of logging in to Stripe, my bank, Gmail, and 30 other SaaS tools, I simply got everything I needed through an AI agent connected to all of them via MCPs.
I wake up, open one chat, and say, "Show me yesterday's revenue, any failed payments, and all questions and bug reports from my customers." Done. No 14 tabs. No context switching. No remembering which dashboard has which chart.
The key reason I chose the products I did was their UX. On the database level, all products are pretty much the same, or even if different, they cover the same use cases.
The Traditional UI was basically a layer between me and the database, and now I can use AI Chat to do reads/writes/updates/deletes.
In cases where I wanna see a chart or a table or anything advanced, I ask AI for it, and it either generates it on the fly or I ask it to generate a little tool for this use case. So I ended up vibe coding a super dashboard that works literally with all the tools I use, all in one, only the things I need.
Here's the thing most people don't realize: I only use 5% of any given tool. The rest was just noise to me. So I just picked the 5% from each and put it all into one dashboard.
Think about that. 95% of every SaaS product you're paying for is features you'll never click on. And now you don't have to.
The UX Moat Is Dead
All this made me think: if UX isn't a MOAT anymore, then I don't really care who is the provider of the API...
Whoever gives me a better price for any of the tools, I'll take it.
I'd even want my agent to take care of this: every day it'd check if there are cheaper alternatives to the tools I use, and if there are, it'd cancel the existing tool, download all my data, and transfer it to the new tool.
No migration headaches.
This obviously means that all such tools gonna have their race to the bottom and reach price zero. And I wouldn't even know what tools I'm using. Their brands wouldn't stick in my mind, because I don't care...
I don't care if my email goes through Resend or Postmark or SES. I don't care if my database is Supabase or PlanetScale or Neon. I just care that the thing works and costs the least.
Brand loyalty is gone. Switching costs are gone.
What's The MOAT Then?
Perhaps the LLM models. Opus 4.6 beats everything else right now, so I'd not switch and I'm willing to pay whatever they ask.
But that game is for the big guys, so it's out of my and your mind as founders.
What's left then?
At first glance... nothing, actually. Tech has no moat. Tech has no defensibility anymore.
Tech business is basically going from 99% profit margins to 1%.
Every SaaS tool is becoming a commodity. Every API is interchangeable. Every database does the same thing. And the AI agent sitting in front of all of them doesn't care about your landing page, your onboarding flow, your clever feature naming, or your freemium funnel.
If you're building a CRUD app with a pretty UI and calling it a SaaS business... yeah, you should be worried. That era is ending.

WHAT COMES NEXT!

The Agent Is The Product
Think about what I did. I collapsed 30 tools into one chat interface. I built my own command center. I only see the 5% that matters to me from each tool.
Cool. But I'm a technical founder who's been shipping products for years. What about everyone else?
Someone needs to do this for them. The agent layer. The orchestration layer.
I basically built this for myself already. The question is: can you productize it?
I think you can. And I think that's where the next wave of billion-dollar companies comes from.
Full repos with one-time payment
For most of my vibe coding projects, I'd happily pay someone $199 to purchase the code that I'll then throw into Claude Code and ask to customize it for my needs, and basically, instead of starting from zero, I start with a good default.
The market for such code repositories will be pretty big. The recurring payment will be gone, but selling 10k copies for $199 each every month will equal $2M/mo, and it's pretty achievable (since most users gonna stop paying monthly for their saas, paying once for such repos gonna be an easy decision)
Big Data (this buzzword finally means smth)
Here's something interesting I noticed. My agent can switch me from Resend to SES in 5 minutes. It can move my database from Supabase to Neon overnight. It can cancel my Stripe account and set me up on a competitor.
But it can't download the collective intelligence of millions of users and bring it with me.
Like... Stripe's fraud detection. It's trained on billions of transactions across millions of businesses. That data IS the product. My agent can't just replicate that by switching to a cheaper payment processor.
If your product genuinely gets better because more people use it... that's the one thing agents can't commoditize. Everything else has no MOAT.
Boring Regulated Stuff
My agent auto-switches my email provider without me knowing. Would I let it auto-switch my bank? My healthcare provider? My insurance underwriter? NO!
The barrier to entry isn't code, it's licenses, compliance, and trust.
Sell The Result, Not The Tool
I don't wanna pay $49/month for a social media management tool anymore. I used maybe 3 features out of 200. And now my agent does those 3 things anyway.
But what I WOULD pay for: "we'll get you 50 qualified leads this month from LinkedIn." That's not a tool, that's a result. And I don't care how you do it. I don't care what APIs you use behind the scenes. I just care that it works.
The pricing model flips from "pay for access" to "pay for outcomes."
You can actually charge MORE than SaaS. Because you're not competing on features or price. You're competing on "did it work or not."
My agent will optimize my tool spending to zero. But it'll happily pay for results all day long, because results have a clear ROI.
The Punchline
Yeah, tech margins are going to zero. For most SaaS, the game is over.
But every time I've seen something die in tech, the thing that replaces it is 10x bigger. Websites killed newspapers, but created Google. Mobile killed websites, but created the app economy. AI agents are killing SaaS, but they're creating... something we don't even have a name for yet.
So stop building tools. Start building what comes after.

It is storing:

a time-series ledger of public business truth.

Every edit = event
Every publish = event
Every correction = event

Current menu = just latest snapshot of that ledger.

The moat is in the ledger.

2. High-level architecture
   You need three layers:

Layer A — Current state (fast read layer)
Used by product UI + rendering.

Collections:

stores

menus

categories

items

storeSettings

Optimized for:

fast reads

rendering

editing

This already exists.

Layer B — Immutable event ledger (core moat)
Append-only change tracking.

This is most important layer.

Every meaningful change → append log entry
Never overwrite
Never delete
Never compress (except deep archive later)

Layer C — Derived intelligence layer (future)
Not now.

Later used for:

reliability scoring

behavioral models

global intelligence

agent trust weighting

For now: just accumulate raw events.

3. Event-first architecture (critical shift)
   Stop thinking:

save final state

Start thinking:

record event → then update state

Every mutation must generate event.

Example flow:

User edits item price
→ create event log entry
→ update item document
Event log is primary truth history.

4. Core event types to implement
   Keep tight and structured.

A. Item-level events (most important)
item_created
Fields:

storeId

itemId

categoryId

initial price

timestamp

source (manual/import/AI)

item_updated
Trigger whenever:

name change

price change

description change

image change

availability change

Store:

changedFields (array)

oldValues

newValues

timestamp

item_deleted
Track:

timestamp

last known price

lifetime duration

item_restored (if happens)
B. Price history events (separate explicit log)
Even if inside item_updated, create explicit price event.

item_price_changed
Fields:

storeId

itemId

oldPrice

newPrice

currency

timestamp

Why separate?
Price dataset becomes extremely valuable later.

C. Availability events
item_availability_changed
Track:

available → unavailable

unavailable → available

temporary disable

timestamp

This builds:

real-world availability behavior dataset.

D. Category/menu structure events
category_created
category_deleted
category_renamed
item_moved_category
menu_reordered
section_restructured
Store:

before

after

timestamp

This builds:

global menu structure dataset.

E. Publish events (critical)
Every publish must log.

menu_published
Fields:

storeId

menuVersion

itemsChangedCount

categoriesChangedCount

timestamp

publishSource (manual/auto)

publish_correction
If publish followed by quick fix:

timestamp

correction type

This reveals:

operational discipline + reliability.

F. Store-level truth events
hours_changed
phone_changed
location_changed
store_status_changed
Track all.

This becomes:

business reliability graph.

5. Firestore schema design (practical)
   Use flat event collections for scalability.

Option A — single global event stream (preferred)
menuEvents (collection)

docId: auto
{
tId
sId
entityType: "item" | "category" | "store" | "menu"
entityId
eventType: "item_price_changed"
oldValue: {}
newValue: {}
changedFields: []
timestamp
actor: "owner" | "ai" | "system"
source: "editor" | "import" | "api"
}
Benefits:

simple

scalable

queryable globally

future ML ready

Option B — per-store subcollection (acceptable)
stores/{storeId}/events/{eventId}
Better for isolation.
Harder for global analysis later.

Recommendation:
Use global event collection + storeId index.

6. Snapshot versioning (important)
   Every publish should create:

menu_snapshot
menuSnapshots
{
storeId
version
fullMenuJson (compressed)
itemCount
timestamp
}
Why:
Lets you reconstruct:

menu at any point in time

historical accuracy

evolution patterns

Snapshot frequency:
Only on publish (not every edit).

Cost manageable.

7. Reliability tracking fields
   Maintain internal store stats doc:

storeTruthMetrics
{
storeId
lastMenuUpdate
lastPublish
priceChangeFrequency
availabilityChangeFrequency
correctionFrequency
publishFrequency
completenessScore
}
Updated via nightly job.

Used later for:

reliability scoring.

8. Cost control strategy
   Event logs will grow large.

But text-based logs are cheap in Firestore if structured lean.

Rules:

keep payload small

store only changed fields

avoid full menu duplication except snapshots

archive >18 months to cold storage (future)

Expected scale:
Even 10M events manageable.

This is worth storing forever.

9. Query capability to preserve (future)
   Design so you can answer later:

Per store:

price volatility

menu stability

update discipline

correction rate

Global:

avg price change per cuisine

seasonal trends

menu size patterns

availability patterns

reliability distribution

If you cannot query these later → tracking insufficient.

10. Non-negotiable technical rules
1. Never overwrite history
   Always append.

1. Every mutation generates event
   No silent edits.

1. Timestamps must be precise
   Use server timestamp only.

1. Actor tracking required
   owner / ai / system / import

1. Version everything
   menuVersion increment.

1. Keep schema stable
   This becomes long-term dataset.

1. What NOT to build now
   Do not build:

analytics UI

insights engine

dashboards

recommendations

reporting

Just accumulate.

Use only for:
internal validation + future moat.

12. Final architecture mindset
    You are building:

the historical ledger of global SMB menu reality.

If done correctly for 3–5 years:

No competitor can recreate:

your price history graph

your menu evolution graph

your reliability graph

your availability graph

That dataset becomes the core defensible asset.

Everything starts with technically correct event tracking.

Conv 8:

You said:
https://youtube.com/watch?v=4Wyy1YiMWwo&si=VFWRtLfzdahgNwek

this video, Adam Robinson shares a step-by-step process for validating an AI startup idea to avoid building something nobody wants (0:35). He emphasizes that intuition is a poor indicator of market demand (2:23) and shares his past failures as examples of not validating ideas effectively.

Here's the framework he recommends:

• Start with conversations, not code (2:43):
• Develop a "sizzling one-liner" that clearly describes the problem you're solving (3:07). For RB2B, it was "person-level website visitor identification" (3:21).
• Conduct hundreds of discovery calls (3:36) to understand what people want and how much they'd pay.
• Key questions to ask during these calls include: Do they understand the problem? Do they have the problem? How are they solving it today? Would they pay for a solution? What else would the solution need to do? What would they pay for it? (5:14).

• Get people to pay before you build (6:20):
• Positive feedback is not validation; money is (6:22). Even a dollar is powerful (6:38).
• Look for "magic moments" – instances where potential customers show genuine excitement or a strong reaction to your proposed solution (7:28). For RB2B, it was real-time Slack notifications of website visitors with LinkedIn profiles (6:56).
• The goal is to reach a point where users would be "livid" if you took the product away (7:41).

• Build the absolute minimum viable product (MVP) (7:52):
• Be ruthless about keeping your product lean and avoid unnecessary features (8:00).
• Stay manual as long as possible, even if it's inefficient (8:08). This allows for more customer interaction and valuable insights. RB2B manually onboarded beta testers for five months before building a website (8:21).
• Focus on the smallest possible niche to dominate it (8:53).

• Test pricing with real users (9:39):
• Don't rely on your gut; test pricing actively (9:52).
• Robinson shares how being transparent about RB2B's initial low conversion rates (10:13) and getting feedback from his LinkedIn audience helped them fix their pricing, leading to a significant increase in free-to-paid conversions (10:34).

• Know when you've achieved product-market fit (11:08):
• Many founders confuse early traction with product-market fit (11:11).
• Real product-market fit is characterized by: strong word-of-mouth, high customer retention, exponential growth even without active marketing, users enduring a horrible user experience but loving the product, and customers being livid if the product were taken away (12:28).
• Until these signals are present, stay in validation mode, keep iterating, and continue doing things manually (13:07).

The video concludes by highlighting that validation is the foundation for successful growth (13:19).
